RAJYA SABHA 
Tuesday, the 16th December, 2025/25 Agrahayana, 1947 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
REFERENCE BY THE CHAIR 
Fifty-Fourth Anniversary of Vijay Diwas 
MR. CHAIRMAN : Hon. Members, today, we commemorate the 54 th anniversary of 
Vijay Diwas. On 16 th December, 1971, the valour, determination and the steadfast 
resolve of our courageous Armed Forces le d Bharat to a historic victory against 
Pakistan. It culminated in the birth of th e independent nation of Bangladesh. This 
decisive victory altered the geopolitical  landscape of our region. But, more 
importantly, it affirmed India’s unwavering commitment to justi ce, human dignity and 
freedom. The 13-day war showcased India’ s military brilliance which was marked by 
swift strategy, seamless coordination and fearless execution by the Indian Army, 
Indian Air Force and Indian Navy. The exem plary courage and bravery, sense of duty 
and unmatched professionalism of our Armed Forces will continue to inspire millions 
of citizens of the country, especially the younger generations to always place the 
nation above oneself. On this solemn occasion of the 54 th Vijay Diwas, the Chair and 
the Members of the House join the fellow co untrymen in paying homage to the brave 
hearts who made supreme sacrifice in the service of the nation. May the legacy of 
their courage and valour guide us today in  our collective resolve to safeguard the 
sovereignty and the integrity of Bharat!   
SHRI JAIRAM RAMESH (Karnataka) : Sir, who was the Prime Minister at that 
time?  …(Interruptions)…  Sir, who was the Prime Minister!  
MR. CHAIRMAN: Now, Papers to be laid on the Table.

Demand for establishing an NTA-led indigenous English Proficiency Examination for 
students seeking overseas education 
SHRI SUJEET KUMAR (Odisha): Sir, since my Zero Hour submission is related to the 
education sector, I begin by complimenting the Education Minister, Shri Dharmendra 
Pradhan, and the Education Ministry for reac hing two major milestones. First, in the 
recent QS World University Rankings, we now have 54 higher education institutes. 
This is five times the number since 2015 an d double the number since last five years. 
Secondly, our premier institutes such as  the IITs and the IIMs now have expanded 
India's education footprints abroad by setting up campuses in Tanzania, UAE and 
now soon in the US.  
With this background, I now draw the attention of the Government to a matter 
affecting lakhs of Indian students going abroad for higher studies, particularly to the 
English speaking world, US, UK, Canada, New Zealand and Australia. Every year, 13 
lakh Indian students sit for English proficiency tests like IELTS and TOEFL and pay 
over Rs.3,000 crores to entities such as the British Council and the Education Testing 
Service (ETS) which conducts these tests. IELTS and TOEFL cost about Rs.20,000 
per attempt. This is a significant financial burden for any middle class student. Several 
countries have already built their own En glish proficiency assessments and have 
secured international recognitions for them . For example, Canada has developed the 
Canadian Academic English Language (CAEL) accepted by leading universities 
globally. Japan’s EIKEN is recognized by hundreds of institutions in US, UK and 
Australia. Japan conducts College Englis h Test (CET) and TEM. South Korea has 
created TEPS. These examples  show that nations have developed domestic English 
testing services and have negotiated their acceptance internationally. But we are yet 
to develop such a mechanism. There is no  reason why India cannot do so. We have 
the infrastructure, we have the expertise, we have the capability and we have proven 
it. Every year we conduct tests for 13 lakh JEE students and 24 lakh NEET students.   
So, Sir, through you, I urge the Governme nt to establish an English proficiency 
assessment conducted by the National Testing Agency (NTA) aligned with 
international benchmarks and ensure global r ecognition. It must be priced affordably 
with full waiver for economically weaker students and should be  conducted in every 
district headquarters nationwide. Also, ME A, in its diplomatic engagement, should 
ensure that these tests scores are recognized by international universities. Sir, this is 
about educational sovereignty, our national respect. It is about saving Rs. 3,000 crore 
every year and it is also about building Atmanirbhar Bharat. Thank you, Sir.  
26
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

MR. CHAIRMAN:  The following hon.  Members  associated  themselves with the 
matter raised by hon. Member,  Shri Sujeet Kumar: Shrimati Sudha Murty 
(Nominated), Shri Kanad Purkayastha (A ssam), Shrimati Mahua Maji (Jharkhand), 
Shri Neeraj Shekhar (Uttar Pradesh), Shri A. A. Rahim (Kerala), Shri Pradip Kumar

Demand for special package and mission-mode programme for all-weather road 
connectivity in Odisha 
SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha) : Thank you, hon. Chairman, Sir, for giving me an 
opportunity to speak on demand for sp ecial package and mission-mode programme 
for all-weather road connectivity in the State of Odisha. Sir, it is deeply distressing 
that around 8,300 revenue villages and more than 5,000 non-revenue forest villages in 
the State of Odisha still remain without basic road connectivity. In the 21
st century, 
when the nation marches confid ently towards the vision of Viksit Bharat , such a 
situation reflects a painful development gap that demands immediate national 
attention.   
Road connectivity is not merely an infrastructure component, it is the 
fundamental right of every ci tizen--an enabler of economic growth, social inclusion, 
health access, education and dignity of life.  In these cut-off and remote habitations, 
people still walk miles through forests, hills  and rivers even fo r primary healthcare, 
schooling of children, essential services , and emergency needs. The absence of 
roads has severely hampered livelihood opportunities, market access, and mobility of 
tribal and rural communities. Sir, these village s, rich in natural resources and cultural 
heritage, remain disconnected  from mainstream development solely due to lack of 
and dependable road communication.  
I, therefore, urge upon the Union Government to take up this matter on a 
priority basis to formulate a special package and mission-mode programme for 
providing all-weather road connectivity to th e zero connectivity villages of Odisha at 
the earliest, ensuring justice, dignity, an d development for my Odisha State. Thank 
you, Sir.

s h o u l d  a l s o  b e  k e p t  i n  m i n d .  I n  2 0 2 4 ,  9 50 acres of land at Palvoncha was further 
identified and we requested the Airports Au thority of India to conduct a pre-feasibility

very region. This would improve the connectivity for business, facilitate tourism for the

SHRI SADANAND MHALU SHET TANAVADE (Goa): Mr. Chairman, Sir, I wish to

20645 and 20646 currently run only betw een Madgaon and Mangalore. Many

Kerala, are facing difficulty as they have  to depend on alternate connections and 
change trains. This often results in long wa iting hours and non-availability of tickets, 
causing hardship to frequent travellers, es pecially senior citizens and tourists. The

present Vande Bharat service between Madgaon and Mangalore is functioning very

[ 16 December, 2025 ]
33

First, the Gopalpur-Rayagada new railway  line must move expeditiously from 
survey to implementation. This approximately 250-kilometre corridor passes through 
Gopalpur, Digapahandi, Bhismagiri, Mohana, and Adava, and will establish a vital link 
between the coastal belt and the tribal hi nterland, connecting with major junctions 
such as Gunupur and Rayaga da, and integrating these re gions into the national 
railway network. Second, the Naupada-Gu nupur-Thiruvali second railway line is 
extremely crucial to ease co ngestion on existing routes. Passengers travelling 
towards Paralakhemundi, Gunupur, and Kora put continue to face overcrowding and 
capacity constraints, and this second line will significantly enhance their comfort and 
operational efficiency. Third, the prop osed Gopalpur-Digapahandi-Taptapani-
Mohana-Padamapur-Singapur road multimod al rail corridor can transform regional 
connectivity by connecting remote and underserved areas such as Taptapani, 
Chandragiri, and Padampur with Singapur ro ad junction, thereby effectively bridging 
the coastal and interior economies of South Odisha. 
Fourth, the long-pending Berhampur-Sambalpur railway line via Hinjili, Aska, 
Bhanjanagar, and Phulbani must be taken up on priority. This crucial route will finally 
place Kandamal district on India's railway map and establish a direct rail link with 
Ganjam, substantially reducing travel hardsh ip faced daily by thousands of citizens.  
Fifth, the Jaipur-Nabarangpur and Jaipur-M alkangiri railway lines have already been 
sanctioned, but they need to be expedited. It is extremely crucial for the integrated 
development of Koraput, Nabarangpur, an d Malkangiri. Finally, Brahmapur railway 
station, the principal rail hub of South Od isha, requires modernisation, additional 
platforms, and a dedicated coaching terminal . I, therefore, urge the Government to 
take up all of these projects expeditiously on  behalf of the people of South Odisha.   
Thank you, Sir.

Need for an effective system to ensure Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) of MSP and 
Market Price difference to farmers 
SHRI IRANNA KADADI (Karnataka): & Hon’ble Chairman Sir, the Government of 
India has announced the Minimum Support Price (MSP) for approximately 23 crops 
with the objective of safeguarding farmers’ intrests against price fluctuations in the 
open market. This important initiative of the Central Government serves as a 
protective mechanism to ensure financial security for farmers. However, when market 
prices fall below the MSP, it is the respon sibility of the respective State Governments 
to open procurement centres and purchase farmers’ produce at the MSP declared by 
the Central Government. 
In Karnataka, during the Kharif season, crops were cultivated over 48.7 lakh 
hectares approximately, including ma ize, soybean, greengram, blackgram, 
groundnut and toor dal. Unfortunately, due to excessive rainfall and flooding during 
this Kharif season, nearly 14 lakh hectares of crops have been damaged, pushing 
farmers into severe financial distress. At such a critical juncture, the State 
Government, which was expected to come to the rescue of the farmers by initiating 
procurement operations, has failed to do so. 
It is deeply distressing to note that 2,809 cases of farmer suicides have been 
reported during the two and a half year s of the Congress Government’s rule in 
Karnataka. Instead of addressing  this grave agrarian crisis, * while farmers continue 
to suffer due to neglect and inaction by th e Government.  As a result of the State 
Government’s failure and delays in procurement, farmers have been compelled to sell 
their Kharif produce at distress prices, furthe r aggravating their financial hardships. In 
this scenario, it is imperative that the Ce ntral Government intervenes, at least for the 
forthcoming Rabi season, to provide much-needed support to the farmers. 
I request that procurement be undertake n through the National Agricultural 
Cooperative Marketing Federation of India Li mited (NAFED), an institution which falls 
under the Central Government. The Central Government already is already procuring 
pulses, oilseeds, dry coconut, and other commodities when market prices fall below 
MSP through the Price Support Scheme (PSS).  Further, I urge the Central

Government to consider implementing the Price Deficiency  Payment Scheme 
(PDPS), whereby the difference  between the MSP and the pr evailing market price is 
directly transferred to farmers’ bank accounts 
Given the exceptional circumstances faced  by farmers in Karnataka this year, 
the Central Government’s intervention is cruc ial. In view of the failure of the State 
Government and the helpless condition of farmers, Chairman Sir, through you, I 
appeal to the Central Government, to take  immediate steps to support and protect 
the farmers of Karnataka. Thank you, Sir.

Concern over the rising suicide among newly married women in the country 
SHRI HARIS BEERAN (Kerala): Mr. Chairman , Sir, I thank you for giving me the 
opportunity.   Sir, a society is judged not by how it celebrates marriage, but by how it 
protects its women after marriage.  Today, I rise to draw the attention of this House to 
a deeply disturbing and painful reality of the rising incidents of suicide among newly 
married women. The NCRB's figure for 2023 reports an alarming 1,71,418 suicidal 
deaths, and majority of them attributed to marriage-related issues. This is not merely 
a social issue; it is a moral, legal and institutional failure that demands urgent 
introspection and decisive action. Sir, as we all know, in Indian tradition, the marriage 
has always been considered a sacred m ilestone, a new beginning, a promise of 
stability, dignity and companionship. Yet for an alarming number of women, marriage 
has become a phase of silent suffering, isolation and despair. A woman after marriage 
steps into an entirely new environment, new relationships, new expectations and 
often unspoken pressures. But, Sir, when  support system fails, when harassment 
replaces harmony, when dowry demands re place dignity and when emotional neglect 
replaces companionship, the consequences are devastating.  
Sir, we are not lacking laws in India. We have got the strongest legal provisions 
in the world to protect married women. But th e harsh truth is that we have failed in 
implementation. In some cases, we have al so witnessed misuse. But I would like to 
suggest here, Sir that we have to amend th e Special Marriage Act so as to include a 
36
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

mandate of pre-marital counselling and certi fication before ma rriage and also the 
Personal Marriage Law. We must also address the healthcare dimension of this crisis. 
Dowry-related harassment remains the si ngle most common cause of unnatural 
deaths among newly married women, de spite decades of legislation. Lower 
education levels of brides, economic stress within the families and maladjustments in 
new households are all reasons. But, Sir, convictions remain low, investigations slow, 
and preventive mechanisms are weak. Therefor e, what we have to do is:  Mental and 
physical health, which has to be impart ed, cannot be ignored. Hormonal changes 
during mensturation, pregnancy, and lactat ion significantly affect the emotional well-
being. Lack of sensitivity, emotional neglect or marital disharmony during these 
vulnerable periods often pushes women to wards extreme steps. Therefore, family 
awareness, counselling and community-based support systems are urgently needed. 
Sir, each suicide is not just the loss of on e woman; it is the collapse of a family, the 
trauma of parents, the lifelon g scar on children and a failu re of the State. Laws on 
paper alone will not save lives. What we need  is empathetic enforcement, institutional 
accountability and social reform backed by political will. जुÊम िसफर्  मार-पीट से ही नहीं

Concern over the working conditions of women frontline workers and vacancies  
under central schemes 
SHRIMATI SONIA GANDHI (Rajasthan): Hon. Chairman, Sir, thank you for giving me 
the opportunity to take up an important issue. I rise to draw the urgent attention of 
this House to the continued distress of  women workers implementing critical 
Government programmes, namely, ASHA work ers, Anganwadi workers and helpers, 
and the Community Resource Persons under the National Rural Livelihood Mission.  
Sir, these initiatives are intended as pa thways to women's empowerment. However, 
despite their vital contribution to public  service delivery, these women workers are 
overburdened and underpaid.  
Across the country, ASHA workers under take immunization, mobilisation, 
maternal health and family welfare. Yet, they remain volunteers with low honoraria and 
limited social security. Anganwadi work ers are similarly paid a meagre base 
honorarium of Rs. 4,500 and Rs. 2,250 per month by the Union Government.   
Sir, beyond low pay, there are currently near 3 lakh vacancies in the ICDS at 
various levels. These gaps leave lakhs an d lakhs of children and mothers without 
essential services.  Even when filled, these posts fall short of population norms due to 
the lack of updated Census figures since 2011.  
Sir, through you, I urge the Government to work closely with the States to 
prioritize the following measures. One, fillin g of all existing vacancies; two, timely 
remuneration to all workers; three, doublin g of Union Government's contribution to 
the pay of these frontline workers; four, a ppointment of an additional ASHA worker in 
villages with a population greater than 2,50 0; and fifth, doubling of the number of 
Anganwadi workers to enable early childh ood education in addi tion to existing 
nutrition and health initiatives.  Sir, I wish to emphasize that strengthening, expanding 
and supporting this workforce is an investment in India's future. Thank you.

   Concern over the hardships faced by OBC community in access to higher education 
SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu): Sir, it has been eight long years. Eight Union 
Budgets have come and gone. The cost of living has skyrocketed. Inflation has 
eroded the value of money and economic cond itions have changed dramatically. Yet, 
the income ceiling for determining the OBC creamy layer remains stuck at eight lakh 
rupees per annum, a limit set back in 2017,  when gold price was Rs. 3,000 per gram.  
It has now escalated to Rs. 12,000 per gram, a 400 per cent increase. The ceiling 
should be revised every three years.  We  are now over two revisions overdue. The 
National Commission for Backward Classes has recommended increasing the ceiling, 
but the Government has not done it. Also, the Department of Personnel and Training 
has a deeply discriminatory practice of calcu lating an individual’s family income by 
including salary. This illegality is followe d in the public sect or undertaking and 
educational institutions, and it is also do ne by the UPSC. Such practices contradict 
the 1993 Office Memorandum, which clearly st ates that salary and agricultural income 
should be excluded from the computatio n of creamy layer. This causes severe 
hardships for candidates trying to avail th emselves of reservation and appointment 
made through UPSC exams and in educatio nal institutions. The OBC Parliamentary 
Committee, in its 2019 report on the ration alization of creamy layer, condemned this 
practice and urged the Government to stop it immediately. However, no action has 
been taken.  
 Now, let us consider the alarming situation caused by this illegality.  Due to 
the ceiling, about 80 per cent of OBC Professor positions remain vacant in Central 
Universities. Out of the 2,537 sanctioned Professor posts in Central Universities, a 
mere 423, that is, 16.67 per cent belongs to OBC. Where is the constitutionally 
mandated 27 per cent? According to the RTI responses received from 21 IITs, 13 IIMs 
and 21 IITs, only about 11.2 per cent of th e faculty belong to the OBC category, six 
per cent are to Scheduled Castes and a mere  1.6 per cent to the Scheduled Tribes.  
In the 13 IIMs, the figures are even more dismal. Just 9.6 per cent of the faculty is 
40
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

OBC, five per cent SC, and just one per cent ST.  In, at least, two IITs and three IIMs, 
the General category holds about 90 per cent of  the faculty positions.  In eight IITs 
and seven IIMs, over 80 per cen t of the faculty is from th e General category. What is 
this? Are IIMs an exclusive club? The impact of the ceiling on the staffing pattern of 
the Union Government is stark. Current Gr oup ‘A’ officers comprise only 18.07 OBC 
representation. Among the 90 top bureaucr ats in the Union Government, only three 
are from OBC community and hold less impo rtant departments. Out of 322 officers 
under Central staffing teams in different mini stries, 16 belong to SC, 13 belong to ST, 
39 belong to OBC, and 254 belong to th e Upper Castes.  Moreover, SC, ST, OBC 
candidates are often ingeniously rejected through a manipulative method called as 
‘candidates not found suitable’ during interviews or through a manipulative process...

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Sustainability of Fair Price Shops 
*1 6 6 .      S H R I SANDOSH KUMAR P: Will the Minister of CONSUMER AFFAIRS, 
FOOD AND PUBLIC DISTRIBUTION be pleased to state: 
 
         (a)  the State-wise list of  Fair Price Shops in the country as on 30th 
September 2025; 
         (b)  whether the digitisation of PDS has led to closing down of Fair Price 
Shops in the country; 
          (c)  whethe r Government has taken any st ep to enhance the financial 
viability of Fair PriceSho ps in the country; and 
          (d)  if  so, the details thereof? 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF CONSUMER AFFAIRS, FOOD AND 
PUBLIC DISTRIBUTION (SHRIMATI NIMUBEN JAYANTIBHAI BAMBHANIYA): (a) to 
(d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a)  The State-wise number of Fair Price Shops in the country is at 
Annexure.  
42
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

(b) to (d)   Targeted Public Distributi on System (TPDS) under National Food 
Security Act (NFSA), 2013 is operated under the joint responsibility of the Central and 
the State/Union Territory (UT) Governments.  
Central Government is responsible for procurement, allocation and 
transportation of foodgrains upto the designated depots of the Food Corporation of 
India (FCI). The operational responsibilities for allocation of foodgrains within the 
States/ UTs, identification of eligible beneficiaries, issuance of ration cards to them, 
distribution of foodgrains to eligible bene ficiaries under TPDS, issuance of license to 
Fair Price Shop dealers, supe rvision over and monitoring of  functioning of FPSs etc. 
rest with the concerned State/UT Governments. 
Accordingly, under sub-clause (7) of cl ause 9 of the TPDS (Control) Order, 
2015, it is the responsibility of the State Government to fix fair price shop owner’s 
margin, and this shall be periodically reviewed for ensuring sustained viability of the 
fair price shop operations.  Thus, the Cent ral Government has no role in fixing the 
actual rate of fair price shop dealers’ margin or payment to the FPS dealers.   
The Central Government only provides  the assistance to States/UTs for 
meeting the expenditure towards intra-St ate movement & handling of foodgrains 
and fair price shop dealers’ margin unde r the NFSA under the provisions of Food 
Security (Assistance to State Governments) Rules, 2015 that provides for norms of 
expenditure and pattern of central sharing.  The norms of FPS Dealers margin are as 
per the details given below: 
Category of States Component of FPS 
margin

(Rate in rupee per 
quintal) (upto 31st 
March, 2022) 
Revised norms 
(Rate in rupee 
per quintal)

Special category states includes Sikkim, Himachal Pradesh, Jammu & 
Kashmir, Ladakh, Uttarakhand, Andama n & Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep and 
seven north east states.  
The State Governments are free to fix th e actual rates, which can be higher 
than the norms specified in the rules. Central assistance will be limited to the rates 
specified in the Rules or the actual average rates for the State as a whole, at which 
the expenditure was actually incurred by the State Government, whichever is lower. 
Further, the Department has linked the payment of additional dealer margins to 
States/UTs with the requirement that ePoS devices be integrated with e-weighing 
scales. This measure incentivises States/U Ts to adopt the linkage, ensuring that 
beneficiaries receive the accurately measured quantity of their entitled foodgrains.  
As per sub-clause (9) of Clause 9 of the Targeted Public Distribution System 
(TPDS) Control Order, 2015, the State Gove rnment shall allow sale of commodities 
other than the foodgrains distributed under the TPDS at the fair price shop to improve 
the viability of the fair price shop operations. 
It has been the endeavor of the Government to improve the financial viability of 
Fair Price Shops (FPSs) by providing additional business avenues to FPS dealers and 
enhancing beneficiary experience through th e provision of value-added services at 
FPS. To improve the financial viability of F PSs, Government of India had requested all 
State/UT Governments to take up initiatives through FPSs such as providing banking 
services through tie-up with banks/corpor ate Banking Correspondents, banking and 
citizen-centric services of India Post Pa yment Bank (IPPB), Retail selling of small 
(5kg) LPG cylinders, Sale of other commodities/ general store items etc.   
The Department has also partnered with the Ministry of Skill Development & 
Entrepreneurship (MSDE) to enhance the capacity and confidence of Fair Price Shop 
(FPS) dealers by equipping them with essential entrepreneurship and business 
management skills. Under this collaborati on, NIESBUD (an autonomous institute 
under MSDE) has developed a specialised  training programme for FPS dealers 
covering digital and financial literacy, e-commerce, retail management, and basic 
nutrition. To date, approximately 325 FPS dealers across nine States—Rajasthan, 
Uttar Pradesh, Gujarat, Telangana, Karn ataka, Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, Tamil 
Nadu, and Bihar—have undergone this traini ng. As a result, 90 FPS outlets have 
been converted into Jan Poshan Kendras (JPKs) across five cities: Hyderabad, 
Ghaziabad, Jaipur, Ahmedabad, and Indore, improving their commercial viability and 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

community outreach. The Department has fu rther requested MSDE to develop an 
online training module through an e-learning management system to enable skill 
development for all 5.5 lakh FPS dealers across the country.

Anti-Rabies Vaccine 
*168. SHRI HARIS BEERAN: Will the Minister of HEAL TH AND FAMILY WELFARE 
be pleased to state: 
(a)  whether the Ministry has conducted any expert study on the deaths of 
people in the State of Kerala from Rabies despite receiving the Anti-Rabies Vaccine 
(ARV); 
(b)  if so, the details thereof; 
(c)  whether Government is aware of the reasons for increase in Rabies cases, 
if so, the details thereof; 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]
49

(d)  the details of the patient safety risks associated with administering the 
ARV that has not undergone mandatory quality tests; and 
(e)  whether Government ha s any action plan on Imm unisation Policy on ARV?  
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY  
WELFARE (SHRI PRATAPRAO JADHAV): (a)  to (e) A Statement is laid on the 
Table of the House.  
Statement  
(a) to (e)  Yes. The Central team compri sing of Public Health Specialist, Drug 
Inspector, Surveillance Officer, Microbiol ogist, Epidemic Intelligence Service (EIS) 
officer had conducted investigation into su spected human rabies deaths in Kerala 
during year 2022 and 2025.  
The details of investigations are as under:   
 
Investigation of suspected human rabies deaths in Kerala during  
year 2022 and 2025 
The samples of Anti Rabies Vaccine (ARV) & Anti Rabies Serum (ARS) of the 
batches used in these cases were collect ed, tested at Central Drug Laboratory 
Kasuali and found to be of Standard Quality as defined in the Drugs and Cosmetic Act 
1940.  
Rabies vaccine is highly effective when given promptly and correctly after 
exposure. Effectiveness depends on timing and completion of the full schedule. 
Anti-Rabies vaccine is highly effect ive, safe and well tolerated. Other 
biological, robust Quality Assurance mech anisms are in place for ARV and ARS in 
India. Central Drugs Standard Control Or ganization governs manufacturing, import, 
and marketing licenses for ARV & ARS, enforces Good Manufacturing Practices 
(GMP) compliance via regular inspections and monitors production, sales, and 
distribution to prevent shortages. The Central Drug Laboratory (CDL) at Kasauli is 
the National Control Laboratory for testing of Immuno-biologicals (vaccines and 
antisera) meant for human use in India. The laboratory ha s the mandate of National 
50
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Regulations of Vaccines produced indigeno usly for domestic market, immunization 
programme of the Government of India, export and for those imported to the country. 
Batches of ARV & ARS are tested at CDL be fore supplying in the system/market as 
per the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940.  
The States/UTs are advised to ensure  the procurement of quality tested 
ARV/ARS through various guidelines/advisories. 
(Link-https://ncdc.mohfw.gov.in/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/Guidance-for-
Preventing-Shortage-of-ARV-ARS.pdf )   
For anti-rabies vaccination (immunization  using ARV), the National Guidelines 
for Rabies Prophylaxis 2019 are being impl emented under National Rabies Control 
Program in all the States/UTs. 
(Link-https://ncdc.mohfw.gov.in/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/Rabies-
Guidelines.pdf ) 
Health is a State subject. The responsibi lity of strengthening public health care 
system lies with respective State/ Union Territories (UTs) Governments.   
To strengthen surveillance, the States/U nion Territories (UTs) have been 
advised to notify rabies as a notifiable disease. As of now, 26 States/UTs have 
notified rabies in their respective jurisd ictions. The details may be seen at the 
following link:  
(Link-https://ncdc.mohfw.gov.in/wpcontent/uploads/2025/12/Letter-from-
MoHFW-for-Rabies-Notification-to-the-states.pdf ). 
Under National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP) for prevention and control 
of Rabies, strategies includes training on appropriate animal bite management, 
surveillance and inter-sectoral coordination, strengthening surveillance of Animal 
Bites, provision of procurement of Anti-Rabies Vaccine for animal bite victims through 
the National Free Drug Initiative and In formation, Education & Communication (IEC) 
activities and awareness videos have been developed and disseminated to 
State/Union Territories to promote public awareness on rabies prevention and the 
importance of timely Post- Exposure Pr ophylaxis (PEP). IEC resources also 
emphasize community responsibility regard ing stray dog vaccination and responsible 
pet ownership, including the need for vacc ination and registration of both pet and 
stray dogs. The IEC resources may be accessed at the link: 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]
51

 
https://rabiesfreeindia.mohfw.gov.in/resources/uploads/PageContentPdf/16939156
6040.pdf 
A total of 279 Model Anti-Rabies Clinics ha ve been established in the last three 
years, improving access to standardiz ed case management and wound-washing 
facilities. 
Fourteen government diagnostic laboratories across country have been 
supported to enhance rabies diagnosis and laboratory-based surveillance. 
Under the ‘National Rabies Control Progr am’, the "National Action Plan for 
Dog-Mediated Rabies Elimination by 2030"  (NAPRE) was conceptualized and jointly 
launched by the Ministry of Health and Fa mily Welfare in collaboration with the 
Ministry of Fisheries, Animal Husbandry, and Dairying.

hon. Minister has said that they have conducted an extensive study but with regard to 
the answer given after that, I could not make it as to what the actual reason or the

to hon. Member to enlighten the House, at least, on the floor of the House, as to 
what the actual reason for the death of these people is, whether it is 
maladministration of drugs or there is so me problem with the vaccine. What is the 
actual reason? 
Ǜी Ģतावराव जाधव: सभापित महोदय, माननीय सदÎय ने यहाँ पर जो सवाल उपिÎथत िकया है, 
उसके  बारे मȂ मȅ बताना चाहता हूँ।  उन्हȗने यहाँ पर यह कहा है िक रेबीज़ के कारण ये जो मौतȂ हुई

NADDA): Sir, I would like to enlighten th e Member and, through him, to all the 
Members and to the country about the disease of rabies. First of all, we have to 
understand that this is a ve ry fatal disease and we have to take a dog bite very 
seriously because once the virus -- as it is a viral disease -- enters into the nerves, 
there is no remedy for it and it becomes fata l. So, what we have to understand is that 
dog bite has to be taken very seriously and what is needed is timely washing of the 
wound and timely treatment. If the timely tr eatment is given, then, the vaccine or the 
serum is very effective. So, what is importan t is that the dosage should be complete. 
The question which you had asked was ab out Kerala. When we sent the Central 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]
53

team, the record says that we investigated  21 cases. The record of one case was not 
available, so we had 20 cases. Fifteen cases did not have vaccination or had 
incomplete vaccination. So, this was the cause of the death. Three died before 
completing the vaccination because of the fatal exposure. वह दांत इतना गहरा चला 
जाता है िक उसकी वॉिंशग ठीक से न होने के कारण वह fatal हो जाता है। One started the 
vaccination after onset of the symptoms, and as I said, when the symptoms are 
there, frankly speaking, death is confirmed. So, what we have to do is, we have to 
start the dosage in time, proper time and pr oper washing is very important as far as 
the dog bite is concerned. Then, about the rabies death in 2025, when we sent the 
second team for investigation, 15 cases we re listed. Out of them, 14 cases did not 
have vaccination or had incomplete vaccination. One who had completed the 
vaccination died due to the fatal exposure . Again the same thin g happened. Batches 
of vaccines used on both the occasions we re tested by the Central Drug Laboratory 
and all were found to be of standard quality.  So the drug is not the issue. The issue is 
when it is administered, whether the people  are reaching hospitals in time and getting 
their wound cleaned at the right time and whether the vaccination process is 
complete. Another thing that you have as ked is about a certain company and you 
have questioned about that certain compan y. We don’t have the record here about 
that. If you ask about that in a separate question, we will answer it.  As far as AIIMS is 
concerned, for that, you ask a separate question. The question of Dr. John Brittas is 
very dear to me and I will answer accordingly.

Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan 
#*170. SHRIMATI RAMILABEN BECHARBHAI BARA : Will the Minister of HEALTH 
AND FAMILY WELFARE be pleased to state: 
                                                            
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
58
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
(a)  the latest details of antenatal ch eck-ups provided to pregnant women so 
far under thePradhan Mantri Surakshit Ma tritva Abhiyan (PMSMA), State/UT-wise; 
(b)  the steps being taken to ensure timely coverage and follow-up care of 
high-risk pregnancy iden tified under PMSMA; 
(c)  whether any challenges have be en faced in ensuring regular PMSMA 
check-ups in aspirational districts, tribal areas and remote rural areas; 
(d)  if so, the corrective efforts being undertaken to address them; and 
(e) whether Government intends to expand digital tools, availability of 
experts, Public-Private-Partnerships under PMSMA, if so, the details thereof?  
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY 
 WELFARE (SHRIMATI ANUPRIYA PATEL): (a ) to (e) A Statement is laid on the 
Table of the House.  
Statement  
(a)  The State/UT-wise antenatal check-ups provided to the pregnant women 
under Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA) since inception of the 
programme in 2016 is placed at Annexure. 
(b) Under the National Health Mission (NHM), the Government of India has 
undertaken several measures to ensure co mprehensive antenatal care, timely follow-
up, and appropriate management of High-Risk Pregnancies (HRPs) identified under 
the Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA). The key steps are as 
follows: 
 
 PMSMA is based on a fixed day strategy, and it provides free of cost assured 
and quality antenatal check up by a Specialis t/Medical Officer on the 9th day of every 
month.  
 Early identification and clinical management of High-Risk Pregnancies (HRPs) 
through comprehensive antenatal check-ups. 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]
59

 Name-based tracking of every identifi ed High-Risk Pregnancy until 45 days 
after delivery, ensuring healthy outcome for both the mother and the new-born across 
all States/UTs. 
 Provision of three additional antenata l check-ups for all HRPs, screened by 
medical officers /obstetricians to monitor maternal and foetal well-being. 
 Automated SMS alerts to beneficiaries and ASHAs at the time of HRP 
registration, reminders for scheduled visits, and follow-up appointments. 
 Linkage of HRPs to the nearest First Referral Units (FRUs) to ensure safe 
delivery and timely management of complications. 
 Provision of incentives for High-R isk Pregnant Women towards free 
transportation to attend follow-up ANC visits. 
(c) and (d) To address implementation  ch allenges, including social and cultural 
barriers, and to ensure regular coverage  under PMSMA, the Government of India 
(GoI) undertaken the following measures: 
 Provision of additional PMSMA session s beyond the fixed monthly sessions 
held on the 9th of every month, to ensure  complete coverage and saturation of all 
beneficiaries. 
 Capacity-building of Medical Officer s at PHCs/CHCs in Basic Emergency 
Obstetric and Newborn Care (BEmONC) to strengthen clinical competencies and 
improve the quality of antenatal services during PMSMA sessions. 
 Case-based ASHA incentives for mobilizing High-Risk Pregnant Women, 
along with transport incentives for beneficiar ies, to facilitate attendance of scheduled 
follow-up ANC visits. 
 Mobile Medical Units (MMUs) are operational in hard-to-reach and tribal areas 
to deliver essential services such as ante natal check-ups, postnatal care, screening 
and referral of high-risk pregnancy. In a ddition, alternative transport arrangements 
such as Palki, Boat Ambulance, and Bike Ambulance etc. are also in place to improve 
last-mile connectivity.  
 Organization of outreach activities in cluding Ayushman Arogya Shivirs, RCH 
camps, and monthly Village Health, Sanitation and Nutrition Days (VHSNDs) to reach 
60
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

marginalized populations, enhance community  mobilization, and ensure follow-up of 
high-risk pregnancies. 
 Distribution of the Maternal and Ch ild Protection (MCP) Card and Safe 
Motherhood Booklet, providing pregnant wo men with essential information on diet, 
rest, birth preparedness, danger signs, ANC/PNC schedules, and government 
benefit schemes. 
 Intensified Information Education Communication (IEC) and Behaviour Change 
Communication (BCC) efforts through mass media, community-based campaigns, 
and interpersonal communication to raise awareness on the importance of regular 
PMSMA check-ups and institutional care. 
 Active engagement of frontline workers, including Auxiliary Nurse Midwives 
(ANMs), Accredited Social Health Activists (ASHAs), and Community Health 
Officers (CHOs), to promote PMSMA services at the community level and ensure 
timely tracking and follow-up of pregnant women. 
         (e) The Government of India has developed a centralized PMSMA Portal to 
facilitate the engagement of private doctor s and specialists as volunteers under the 
Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA). After registering on the portal, 
their details are shared with the respective State and district authorities. The district 
PMSMA Nodal Officers subsequently co ordinate and communicate the required 
follow-up actions to ensure effective service delivery.

the goods are distributed. Thank you, Chairman, Sir. 
SHRI KANAD PURKAYASTHA: Mr. Chairman, Sir, I want to know from the hon.  
Minister what the existing policy of food grains procurement is and what the scheme 
of decentralized procurement of food grains is.  
70
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
SHRI PRALHAD JOSHI : Sir, the principle of procurem ent of food grains has broad 
objectives, ensuring the availability of food grains to the weaker  sections. And, the 
Central Government extends price support  to paddy, coarse grain, and wheat, 
through the Food Corporation of India and State agencies. The procurement policy is 
open-ended. All food grains conforming to th e prescribed specifications, that is, fair 
average quality norms, are offered for sale at specified centres and are bought by the 
public procurement agencies. The farmers have the option to sell their produce to FCI 
and the State agencies at the support pr ices in the open market, whichever is 
advantageous to them. Additionally, Sir, th e scheme of decentralised procurement of 
food grains was introduced by the Govern ment in 1997-98 with a view to enhancing 
the efficiency of procurement and PDS, and encouraging local procurement to the 
maximum extent by extending th e benefit of MSP to local farmers, as well as to save 
the transit costs.  Furthermore, whenever we decide the quantity to be purchased 
from each State, we consid er factors such as what is sown, expected production 
and, after consultation with the State Government, the quantity is decided.

cities; 
      (b) the number of Cancer cases dete cted in Delhi and Punjab during the last 
three years, Cancer type-wise such as Lu ng, Breast, Oral, Cerv ical and Prostate 
and gender-wise;

programmes implemented by Government in Delhi and Punjab along with the funds 
allocated, released and utilised; and

formulated, the details and timeline thereof?  
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MI NISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY

of the House.  
Statement  
(a) As per the Indian Council of Medi cal Research (ICMR) report, the Age 
Adjusted Incidence Rates (AAIR) of cancer per 100000 (2 015-2019) across

(b) As per the ICMR -National Can cer Registry Programme (NCRP), the 
estimated number of incidence in Delhi and Punjab during the last three years, Cancer 
type-wise such as Lung, Breast, Oral, Ce rvical and Prostate and gender-wise is 
placed at Annexure. 
(c) The Department of Health and Family Welfare, under National Health 
Mission (NHM), provides technical and financial support to States/UTs for the 
National Programme for Prevention and Control of Non-Communicable Diseases 
(NP-NCD). The programme focuses on strengthening infrastructure, human 
resource development, screening, early diag nosis, referral, treatment, and health 
promotion for Non-Communicable Diseases  (NCDs) including cancer. Under NP-
NCD, 770 District NCD Clinics, 233 Cardi ac Care Units, 364 District Day Care 
Centres, and 6,410 NCD clinics at CHCs have been set up. 
A population-based initiative for prevention, control and screening for common 
NCDs including three common cancers has been  rolled out. Under this initiative, 
persons more than 30 years of age are targeted for their screening for the common 
NCDs including three common cancers i.e oral, breast and cervical.  
The preventive aspect of Cancer is  strengthened under Comprehensive 
Primary Health Care through Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAM) by promotion of 
wellness activities. Under National Health Mission (NHM), funds are provided to 
States & Union Territories for awarene ss generation activities as per their 
Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs). 
Since health is a State Subject in addi tion to expenditure by the State, the 
details of expenditure for National Pr ogramme-Non Communicable Diseases (NP-
NCD) under NHM during the period of F.Y. 2022-23 to F.Y. 2024-25 for Delhi and the 
State of Punjab is given as below:-

(d) As per the announcement of Union Budget 2025-26, the government plans 
to set up Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCCs) in  district hospitals in the next 3 years.

In addition, initiatives of other Mini stries/departments also support cancer 
prevention. The Eat Right India movement of the Food Safety and Standards Authority 
of India (FSSAI) encourages reduced intake  of salt, sugar and trans-fats. The Fit 
India movement of the Ministry of Youth A ffairs and Sports, and various Yoga-related 
activities undertaken by the Ministry of AYUSH promote healthier lifestyles, which are 
helpful in reducing risk factors associated with cancer.

Constitution begins with the words ‘We the People’. So, ‘The People’ is the ultimate 
factor in a democracy and we are the mother of democracy. But as far as right to vote 
is concerned, it is not mentioned either as fundamental right or the constitutional 
right.  There have been various judgements of the Supreme Court. Some say that it is 
the statutory right, some say that it is th e constitutional right, while some say that 
voting itself is an integral part and basic feature of the Constitution.  So, there is no 
86
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

clarity about the right of the people to vote.  I would suggest, at this point of time, the 
right to vote should be made a fundamental right in the Constitution itself.   
Now, Sir, the fundamental motto of the Election Commission is: ‘everyone 
should vote’. If our motto is ‘everyone should vote ”, all Indian citizens, who are 
entitled to vote, should be eligible to vote.  We have around 40 crore migrant 
labourers in the country who are unable to vote because they are not in their particular 
native place or the constituency where they have to cast their vote. Similar is the case 
of the Non-Resident Indians who have gon e outside the country for the purpose of 
employment. There have been instances in the past where a Bill was introduced in the 
Lok Sabha, which was passed by the Lok Sabh a, saying that there has to be remote 
voting facility for the Non-Resident Indian s as well as for the migrant labourers. I 
would recommend that this particular category of Indian citizens should be entitled to 
vote. Now, Sir, as far as electoral process is concerned, transparency is the key. If 
there is no transparency, the public will lose faith. There have been several instances 
where in the electoral process transpar ency has lost sight of.  Why did the 
Government scuttle the Supreme Court decisi on that Chief Justice of India should 
also be a part of the committee for elect ing the Chief Election Commissioner?  Why 
was he kept out, Sir? Why is this model Code of Conduct not being made applicable 
fairly to all political parties uniformly? Why are these hate speeches allowed in election 
campaigns? We saw, Sir, 
* There is a provision in the Representation of the People 
Act that if there is a hate campaign, that particular election itself should be made void. 
So, all the elections of these BJP MPs woul d have been void if that particular 
provision under the Representation of the People Act had been invoked. But nothing 
is being invoked, although we have got  the law. Now, Sir, there are peculiar 
situations.  You always see the Election Commission taking a decision and it is the 
BJP coming up and defending that particul ar decision of the Election Commission. 
Sir, they are two different entities alt ogether. Why should the BJP come in? Here 
also, in this discussion, it was the Treasu ry Benches who are up in arms saying that 
the SIR has to be done and whatever the Election Commission is doing is correct.  It 
is not their role. It is the role of the Election Commission to come up and say that, 
yes, we are doing a good role; we are doin g it in a transparent manner.  But then, 
there is no difference as far as the El ection Commission and the BJP are concerned 
from whatever voices we are hear ing from the Treasury Benches.  …( Time-bell 
rings.)…  Sir, just 30 seconds more.

SHRI MOHAMMED NADIMUL HAQUE (West Bengal): Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I 
thank you for giving me the opportunity to speak on this Bill. On behalf of my Party, All 
India Trinamool Congress, I would like to  share with the House three poems by 
Gurudev Rabindranath Tagore to tell what  this Government has missed in this 
Appropriation Bill. 
 Sir, I will start with the first poem, ‘Where the Man is Without Fear’. The poem 
talks about how citizens will live without f ear of the Government bulldozing on their 
rights and hurting their dignity. Are the citi zens living without this fear? On the other 
hand, what do we see? This Bill has no money for MNREGA. This Bill has no money 
for the dues owed to the State of West  Bengal, which are as follows: Rs. 52,000 
crores under MNREGA, Rs. 43,500 crores under various Disaster Relief Funds, Rs. 
19,000 crore under Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan and more than Rs. 2,500 crores under 
Jal Jeevan Mission. In total, over Rs. 2, 00,000 crore are due to the State of West 
Bengal under different scheme s. How can we say the dign ity of the people has not 
been hurt by this Government? How can we  talk about rights not being neglected 
when the z
amindars  have such a Bangla-virodhi mind set? In answer to the questions 
asked by us, they have not given any time lines for the resumption of MNREGA. They 
have committed contempt of court. They have disobeyed the orders passed by the 
High Court and the Supreme Court. 
 Sir, the next poem is ‘Dui Bigha Jomi ’. The poem talks about the plight of a 
marginalized farmer , who lost his two bighas of ancestral lands to the zamindars.  
Today, the zamindars -- I need not say who the zamindars are, everybody knows -- 
have led the country to such a situation th at nearly 30 farmers are committing suicide 
daily. An agricultural labourer earns only about Rs. 266 per day, yet zero amount has 
been given in this Bill for them. In fact, th is Government has given fertile land of our 
farmers to big corporate houses for very few rupees. At one or two rupee rate, 
hundreds of acres of land have been given to  them. Do not forget that nearly half of 
the workforce is engaged in agriculture. Sir, they have failed our Annadatas, they 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]
127

have failed to even double their income.  However, learn from Bengal.  We have not 
only doubled but tripled the farmers’ income.  
I would like to conclude with the third poem ‘Ekla Chalo Re’. The poem has a 
line, and, I quote, “If they answer not to thy call, walk alone. ”  T h a t  i s  w h a t  i s  
happening with the zamindars calling Bangla language Bangladeshi.  
…(Interruptions)… This is what has happened with … …(Interruptions )… You may not 
take the responsibility but our leader, Mama ta Banerjee, will stand by the Maa, Mati, 
Manush. In this Appropriation Bill, the Ministry of Home Affairs has got more than half 
of the entire annual Budget. However, I want to ask where is this money going? Is it 
really going towards national security? Th e Home Minister does not own up to the 
failure in securing the borders. He shifts  the blame on the West Bengal Government. 
The reality is different from what he claims . The responsibility to safeguard the border 
lies with him. In fact, in 2021, the BSF’s jurisdiction was expanded from 15 kms. to 50 
kms. including in West Bengal. The poem  ‘Ekla Chalo Re’ resembles the state of 
youth in this country. One in three youth is not in employment, education or training, 
with women comprising an overwhelming nine out of ten amongst them. 
The youth unemployment rate is at 15  per cent between April and October, 
2025.  The situation is even far worse for the young urban women, as one out of every 
four is unemployed. The much advertised PM Internship Scheme has also failed. Only 
4 per cent of the applicants wh o applied for the internships are actually interning. For

cent. Sir, BJP may try to de prive Bengal by holding our dues, calling us Bangladeshi 
and turning national security into a slogan, but, as I said, ‘Bengal will walk alone’ to 
protect its people, its language, its compos ite culture and the Constitution. ‘Bengal 
will not stop’.  Vande Mataram! Jai Hind! Jai Bangla!

sent me to this august Hous e. I wish to place my record here that I, Mr. Sivalingam, 
shall remain grateful and indebted to such a noble leader for my entire life. 
Sir, in a federal country like India, the Union Budget is not merely a book of 
accounts. It is a document that reflects the political vision of the Government 
regarding equitable development. Through th is speech, I wish to place on record not 
only the concerns of the people of our State, but also the broader demand for 
financial justice for all States within our federal structure.   
Sir, due to the flawed economic policies of the Union Government for the past 
ten years, India’s household savings rate has fallen from 23.6% of GDP to 15.1%. 
Families once relied on fixed deposits and small savings schemes. Now, they are 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]

struggling to meet even their daily expenses. With shrinking employment 
opportunities and rising costs of living, peop le are being forced to take loans just to 
manage basic necessities.  The taxes paid by  the people of India in good faith should 
be used to generate employment and stre ngthen the economy. Instead, there is an 
emergence of rising unemployment and fina ncial distress only. As per the data on 
December 2024, India’s unemployment rate has risen to 8.33%, posing a serious 
threat to the future of Indian youth. The Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment 
Guarantee Act (MGNREGA), the lifeline of rura l employment, is facing a severe crisis 
due to inadequate fund allocation. Funds  allocated are far below actual demand, 
pushing rural workers into financial hardsh ip. Wages for 100-day scheme workers are 
being delayed. The Micro, Small and Me dium Enterprises (MSME) sector, which 
provides employment to over 12 crore peop le and contributes nearly 30% of India’s 
GDP, continues to be largely neglected. 
Sir, in order to economically weaken the States ruled by opposition parties, the 
Union Government is deliberately withhold ing funds due for them. Several States, 
including Tamil Nadu, are forced to face immense financial stress due to delays and 
reductions in tax devolution, GST compen sation, and centrally sponsored scheme 
funds.  The Union Government’s unfair fis cal distribution policies and stringent 
conditions attached to release of funds,  undermine State autonomy. They adversely 
affect administration and economic stability of the States. It sh ows that the Union 
Government prioritizes centralized politi cal control rather than strengthening 
cooperative federalism, an appr oach that goes against the ve ry spirit of the rights of 
the States, which is the lifeline of the Constitution of India.  
Sir, under the leadership of the hon. Chief Minister of Tamil Nadu, the 
Dravidian Model of governance has significantly contributed to India’s GDP by 
strengthening industrial infrastructure and by efficiently implementing social welfare 
schemes across sectors. Yet, the Union Government’s stance on fund allocation 
gives the impression that Tamil Nadu is being penalised for its success. Even the 
present Supplementary Demands for grants refl ect the denial of legitimate rights and 
expectations of States like Tamil Nadu th at contribute substantially to national 
revenue. Despite being a model State for soci al justice, federal rights, and inclusive 
development, Tamil Nadu’s share in Ce ntral tax devolution has been steadily 
reduced. Under the 15th Finance Commission , Tamil Nadu’s share has been reduced 
to a mere 4.79%. This has increased fi nancial strain and has created serious 
challenges even in implementing disaster  relief measures. Tam il Nadu, with an 82% 
literacy rate and a Gross Enrolment Ratio of 51.44% in higher education, stands

among the leading States in educational deve lopment. Yet, for refusing to adopt the

Sir, Supplementary Demands for Grants  usually arise due to unforeseen 
expenditures, new schemes, or disasters not anticipated in the Budget. Over the past 
decade, Tamil Nadu has been repeatedly hit by cyclones  such as Nivar, Vardah, 
Ockhi, and Michaung, causing massive loss of lives, and damage to property, crops, 
and infrastructure. The Tamil Nadu Government requested ₹24,679.7 crore as 
disaster relief from the Union Government. However, over the last ten years, only 
₹4,136 crore—just 17% of the requ ested amount—has been released.

SHRI HARIS BEERAN (Kerala): Thank you, Vice-Chairman, Sir. The Government is 
presenting this Appropriation Bill to authorise Gross Additional Expenditure of Rs.1.32 
lakh crores for the financial year 2025-26. It  is critical to note that the Net Cash 
Outgo, which refers to the actual fresh money leaving the Consolidated Fund, is 
restricted to only Rs.41,000-odd crores. Wh at does this mean? This means that the 
majority of the Bill is not funded by ne w revenue, but by re-appropriating unspent 
funds from the other areas. This is act ually a damning indictment of fiscal 
mismanagement and a very poor Budget pl anning. The remaining Rs.90,000 crore is 
technically classified as savings or technical supplementaries, meaning the 
Government is funding new expenses by ut ilising money that was allocated to other 
Ministries but remains unspent. This points to severe allocative inefficiency and 
execution paralysis, suggesting that deve lopment projects in specific sectors are 
being stalled. We will examine two or th ree Ministries -- one being the Higher

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Education Ministry. Under the Department of  Higher Education, the Government had 
introduced a large new scheme called the Pradhan Mantri One Nation One 
Subscription with a gross provision of  over Rs.2,000 crore; whereas, the 
Supplementary Demands for the Department stands at Rs.1,300 crore. The concern 
is not about the spending of money, but ab out the priorities. For example, Kerala's 
higher education is struggling with shor tages of faculty, inadequate laboratory 
infrastructure, hostel capacity constrai nts and delayed capital work. A highly 
centralised digital subscription sche me cannot substitute for these basic 
requirements. One of the areas  of major concern is the Mi nistry of Minority Affairs. 
There is a historical trend where the Minist ry of Minority Affairs surrenders substantial 
portions of the Budget due to under-utilisat ion. In previous fiscal years, utilisation 
rates have been drastically lower than th e Budget Estimates. If the Ministry is 
contributing to the savings in this Bill,  it implies that the funds meant for the 
educational empowerment of minority students  are being effectively diverted to fund 
deficits in fertilizer or petroleum subsidies.  This is a regressive redistribution taken 
from the most marginalised communities to balance the general subsidy bill. The 
Supplementary Demands fail to include any remedial allocation to clear the persistent 
reports of scholarship to minority students.

A- Contd. 
Motion for nomination to serve on the Joint Committee on the Viksit Bharat  
Shiksha Adhishthan Bill, 2025 
SECRETARY-GENERAL: Sir, with your kind permission, I rise to report that the Lok 
Sabha at its sitting held on the 16 th December, 2025, adopted the following Motion in 
regard to the Viksit Bharat Shiksha Adhishthan Bill, 2025.  
“That the Bill to enable and empower the Universities and other higher 
educational institutions to achieve excelle nce in teaching, learning, research and 
innovation, through coordination and determ ination of standards in institutions for 
higher education or research and scientific  and technical institutions, and for that 
purpose to constitute a Viksit Bharat Shiksh a Adhishthan, to facilitate the Universities 
and other higher educational institutions to become independent, self-governing 
institutions and to promote excellence thro ugh a robust and transparent system of 
accreditation andautonomy, and for matters connected therewith or incidental thereto 
be referred to a Joint Committee of the Ho usesconsisting of 21 Members of this 
House to be nominated by the Hon’ble Speaker Lok Sabha and 10 Members of Rajya 
Sabha to be nominated by Hon’ble Chairman; 
that in order to constitute a sitting of  the Joint Committee the quorum shall be, 
as near as may be, one-third of the total number of Members of the Joint Committee;  
that the Committee shall make a report to this House by the last day of the first 
part of the Budget Session 2026;  
that in other respect, the Rules of Pr ocedure and conduct of Business of this 
House relating to Parliamentary Committees  shall apply with such variations and 
modifications as the Speaker may make; and  
that this House recommends to Rajya Sa bha that Rajya Sabha do join the said 
Joint Committee and communicate to this House names of the Members to be 
appointed by Rajya Sabha to the Joint Committee.” 
[ 16 December, 2025 ]

year2025-26, as passed by Lok Sabha, be taken into consideration.” 
The motion was adopted. 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN : We shall now take up Clause-by-Clause consideration of 
the Bill.  
Clauses 2, 3 and the Schedule were added to the Bill. 
Clause 1, the Enacting Formula and the Title were added to the Bill. 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN : Now, Shri Pankaj Chaudhary to move that the Bill be 
returned.
